The source prose elsewhere names $(u_{10},c)$ as the halting cell, whereas Table 16 defines that cell as $b,L,u_{11}$. This release follows the unambiguous table: $J1$ is undefined and $J0$ is defined. The issue is recorded rather than silently changing a transition.

\wtagc\ Table~\ref{gts:um:tab:machine} is the third printing of this table in the collection. It agrees entry by entry (states $A,\ldots,O$ for $u_1,\ldots,u_{15}$, $0=c$, $1=b$, $J1$ undefined) with Tables \texttt{qoc:wf:tab:tm} and \texttt{qoc:um:tab:tm} of the report \emph{quadratic-orthant-certificates}~\cite{qoc} (its Parts~III and~V) and with the transcription in the research programme's note~\cite{repo-nw15}, added on 1~October 2026. The inconsistency between Table~16 and the prose of Neary and Woods recorded in the preceding paragraph is also recorded in that note; \emph{quadratic-orthant-certificates} prints it in full after its Table \texttt{qoc:wf:tab:tm}. Source~08 cites neither, found the inconsistency independently and resolves it in the same way. Its serialized table \path{core/data/u15_table.json} is byte-identical to \path{data/16-universal-membrane-tm_table.json} of \emph{quadratic-orthant-certificates} and is not shipped here (the README restores it for reruns); all 30 cells of that file were compared with Table~\ref{gts:um:tab:machine} at this write.

\subsection{Exactly what universality supplies}
Let $H_U$ be the set of finite tape pairs from Section~\ref{gts:um:sec:main} on which this fixed table reaches $J1$. All such pairs are legitimate inputs, even when they do not encode a meaningful simulated program. Hardness requires only a well-formed subfamily.

The published reference is \emph{Fundamenta Informaticae} 91(1) (2009), pages 123--144, DOI \href{https://doi.org/10.3233/FI-2009-0036}{10.3233/FI-2009-0036} \cite{um-NW}. The author-hosted PDF used for detailed inspection has draft pagination 105--126 and different draft DOI metadata. Therefore a locator such as ``Table 16, printed page 121'' below refers to that draft PDF, not to page 121 of the published article.

The effective source chain is as follows.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Lemma 2.1, draft pages 108--109, constructs a clockwise machine from an ordinary deterministic one-tape machine. Input conversion is a finite-state transduction; finite boundary markers represent the two infinite blank tails.
\item Lemma 2.2, draft pages 110--111, converts the clockwise machine to a bi-tag system while preserving the simulated halting event. Theorem 2.1 composes these stages.
\item Definition 3.1, equations (3)--(4), and Tables 1--3, draft pages 112--113, specify finite program/data encodings for the universal machines.
\item Theorem 3.1 proves the simulation for $U_{9,3}$; the paragraph after that proof extends the correctness argument to the other machines. Section 3.5 and Table 16 give the $U_{15,2}$ implementation and halting mechanism, draft pages 120--123.
\end{enumerate}
Theorem 3.1 alone is not a separately stated $U_{15,2}$ r.e.-completeness theorem. The cited chain yields that conclusion by effective composition.

For the properly encoded bi-tag configurations produced by these constructions, the finite physical tape has the form $P_{\rm prog}\,b\,[c]\,D_{\rm data}$, where brackets here designate the scanned cell, not tape characters. Taking no optional padding, our two halves are
\[
 \ell=\rev(\operatorname{binary}(P_{\rm prog}b)),\qquad
 \rho=\operatorname{binary}(D_{\rm data}),
\]
with $b\mapsto1$ and $c\mapsto0$. Both tails are blank. The construction supplies a total effective halting-preserving map from valid machine/input codes into this family. Standard effective coding can assign malformed codes to a fixed nonhalting computation. Consequently the ordinary halting set reduces many-one to $H_U$. Direct simulation makes $H_U$ r.e., hence it is many-one r.e.-complete.

This report proves the downstream local construction in full. It does not reprove every step of the published universal simulation, does not materialize its complete arbitrary-TM-to-bi-tag compiler, and does not infer a source-program complexity bound from the finite-tape bound below. An executable finite-tape encoder is a narrower deliverable than an executable arbitrary-program encoder.

\section{A directed rewriting system with 93 rules}
\label{gts:um:sec:rewriting}
Use the ordered alphabet
\[
 \Sigma=(0,1,A,B,C,D,E,F,G,H,I,J,K,L,M,N,O,[,],X).
\]
The brackets are actual letters in this alphabet. The fresh letter $X$ is a cleanup marker and the one-letter terminal word. A live finite configuration is $[\,l\,q\,a\,v\,]$, with $l,v$ binary words, $q$ a state, and $a$ the scanned bit. Unrepresented cells beyond the endpoints are zero. The initial word is
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:initial}
 w(\ell,\rho)=[\,\rev(\ell)\,A\,0\,\rho\,].
\end{equation}

For each defined transition $(q,a)\mapsto(b,R,p)$, include
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:Rrules}
 qa0\hto bp0,\qquad qa1\hto bp1,\qquad qa]\hto bp0].
\end{equation}
For each defined transition $(q,a)\mapsto(b,L,p)$, include
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:Lrules}
 0qa\hto p0b,\qquad 1qa\hto p1b,\qquad [qa\hto[p0b.
\end{equation}
The boundary cases insert one newly visited blank. Finally include
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:clean}
 J1\hto X,\quad 0X\hto X,\quad X0\hto X,\quad
 1X\hto X,\quad X1\hto X,\quad [X]\hto X.
\end{equation}
There are $29\cdot3+1+4+1=93$ rules, all with nonempty sides. Their deterministic enumeration scans transition cells $A0,A1,B0,B1,\ldots,O1$, skips $J1$, and lists the two bit contexts then the boundary context for each cell. It then appends the six rules in \eqref{gts:um:eq:clean}. This convention and Table~\ref{gts:um:tab:machine} specify every rule without an implicit choice.

\begin{lemma}[Exact simulation and cleanup]\label{gts:um:lem:rewrite}
For every finite binary pair $(\ell,\rho)$,
\[
 U\text{ halts on }(\ell,\rho)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 w(\ell,\rho)\st X.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A live configuration contains one state letter. If the cell is defined, its state and scanned bit select one transition, and the symbol on the movement side selects exactly one of its three context rules. No rule of another cell can match. No cleanup rule can match because $X$ is absent. The resulting word is the next live configuration, with exactly the prescribed tape write and head move. At an endpoint the matching boundary rule adds precisely the blank cell the infinite-tape machine next visits.

At the undefined cell $J1$, the only applicable rule involving a state is $J1\hto X$. The result has the form $[lXv]$. The four erasures can delete all binary letters on either side, after which $[X]\hto X$ finishes. Each cleanup step shortens the word. Conversely, the only way to create $X$ is to apply $J1\hto X$, so any derivation to $X$ must first reach the machine's halting cell. Before this event every rewrite was the unique correct simulation step. There is no reverse rewrite, extra rejecting halt, or boundary erasure that could bypass it.
\end{proof}

\section{From rewriting to 114 fixed tiles}
\label{gts:um:sec:tiles}
Introduce a fresh separator $\#$. The ordered tile list $(h_i,g_i)$ consists of 20 copy tiles $(a,a)$ in alphabet order, 93 tiles $(v,u)$ corresponding in order to the directed rules $u\hto v$, and the final separator tile $(\#,\#)$ at index 114. Every side is nonempty; only tile 114 contains $\#$.

For a sequence $s=i_1\cdots i_m$, let $h(s)=h_{i_1}\cdots h_{i_m}$ and $g(s)=g_{i_1}\cdots g_{i_m}$. Empty sequences give empty words.

\begin{lemma}[Exact correspondence equation]\label{gts:um:lem:tiles}
For every word $w\in\Sigma^*$, including malformed configuration words,
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:correspondence}
 w\st X\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists s:\quad w\#h(s)=g(s)X\#.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $w=w_0\hto w_1\hto\cdots\hto w_d=X$. A step $\alpha u\beta\hto\alpha v\beta$ gives a tile block: the copies of $\alpha$, the tile $(v,u)$, the copies of $\beta$, and the separator. Concatenating these blocks yields
\[
 h(s)=w_1\#\cdots w_d\#,\qquad
 g(s)=w_0\#\cdots w_{d-1}\#,
\]
so \eqref{gts:um:eq:correspondence} holds. For the zero-step derivation $w=X$, choose $s=\eps$.

Conversely split the sequence at its $k$ occurrences of tile 114:
$s=s_0\,114\,s_1\,114\cdots114\,s_k$, where no $s_j$ contains that tile. Comparing the final $\#$-separated segments of the equation forces $h(s_k)=\eps$. Nonempty tile sides imply $s_k=\eps$, and hence $g(s_k)=\eps$. If $k=0$, the entire sequence is empty and $w=X$. If $k>0$, the remaining segment equalities are
\[
 w=g(s_0),\quad h(s_0)=g(s_1),\quad\ldots,\quad
 h(s_{k-2})=g(s_{k-1}),\quad h(s_{k-1})=X.
\]
For each $j$, the tiles of $s_j$ partition $g(s_j)$ into disjoint, successive nonempty pieces. Copy tiles preserve their pieces; every other tile replaces one rule's left side by its right side. These disjoint replacements can be performed from right to left, preserving the original positions of all remaining pieces, and give $g(s_j)\st h(s_j)$. Concatenation gives $w\st X$.

This direction allows several rule tiles in one segment and copy-only segments. It does not assume that segment words are machine configurations. For well-formed initial words, Lemma~\ref{gts:um:lem:rewrite} supplies that additional invariant.
\end{proof}

\wtagc\ \textbf{Credit.} Lemmas~\ref{gts:um:lem:rewrite} and~\ref{gts:um:lem:tiles} re-derive, for the same machine, a construction already in the research programme, which source~08 does not cite. The note~\cite{repo-nw15} (its Section~1) compiles $U_{15,2}$ into a bounded-tape rewriting system with $29(2+1)+1+2\cdot2=92$ rules and 113 full-copy tiles (97 after deleting the copies of state letters), and the notes~\cite{repo-gpcp} prove the fresh-delimiter equivalence $\exists w:\ \sigma(w)\#v=u\#\tau(w)\iff u\Rightarrow^*v$, the mirror image of~\eqref{gts:um:eq:correspondence} (tile sides interchanged, delimiter at the other end). The two constructions differ only at the end of a run: the note keeps a halting state and erases the tape on both sides down to the word $[\,\mathit{halt}\,]$, whereas source~08 turns $J1$ into a fresh letter $X$ and also erases the brackets, by $[X]\hto X$, which accounts for its 93 rules and 114 tiles. No novelty is claimed here for the two lemmas; they are printed as source~08's own route. The matrix stage of Sections~\ref{gts:um:sec:matrices}--\ref{gts:um:sec:reduction} has no counterpart in those notes.

\section{The numerical matrices and free embeddings}
\label{gts:um:sec:matrices}
\subsection{A faithful integer encoding}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:Ej}
 P=\smallmat{1}{2}{0}{1},\qquad Q=\smallmat{1}{0}{2}{1},\qquad
 E_j=Q^{-j}PQ^j=\smallmat{1+4j}{2}{-8j^2}{1-4j}.
\end{equation}
Every determinant is one.

\begin{lemma}\label{gts:um:lem:free}
The matrices $P,Q$ freely generate a rank-two free group. Any finite set of distinct $E_j$, including $E_0=P$ if desired, is freely independent.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Use the real projective line with the disjoint sets $\mathcal A=\{|z|>1\}$ (including infinity) and $\mathcal B=\{|z|<1\}$. For every nonzero integer $n$,
\[
 P^nz=z+2n\in\mathcal A\quad(z\in\mathcal B),\qquad
 Q^nz=\frac{z}{2nz+1}\in\mathcal B\quad(z\in\mathcal A).
\]
The inequalities are strict. In the second case $|2nz+1|>|z|$ for $|z|>1$, and infinity maps to $1/(2n)$. Each nonidentity power is nonidentity, so both cyclic subgroups are infinite.

For clarity, the ping-pong conclusion can be seen directly. A reduced alternating word with the same factor type at both ends sends the opposite set into a disjoint set, so is nonidentity. For an alternating word with different end types, conjugate by a suitable nonzero power of its final factor type. Because that cyclic group is infinite, the conjugating exponent can avoid cancellation at that end. The conjugated reduced word then has the same type at both ends and is nonidentity. Pure powers are already nonidentity. Thus no nonempty reduced $P,Q$ word acts trivially; in particular it cannot be the identity matrix.

Now collect any nonempty reduced word in distinct $E_j$ as
$E_{j_1}^{n_1}\cdots E_{j_k}^{n_k}$, with nonzero $n_i$ and adjacent indices unequal. Its expansion is
\[
 Q^{-j_1}P^{n_1}Q^{j_1-j_2}P^{n_2}\cdots
 Q^{j_{k-1}-j_k}P^{n_k}Q^{j_k}.
\]
Every interior $Q$ exponent and every $P$ exponent is nonzero. Deleting any empty endpoint powers leaves a nonempty reduced word in the free generators $P,Q$. This proves the second assertion, not merely abstract freeness of their generated subgroup.
\end{proof}

\wtagc\ $P$ and $Q$ are the Sanov shears $A$ and $B$ of Part~I~\eqref{gts:vk:eq:shears}. The first assertion of Lemma~\ref{gts:um:lem:free} is therefore the injectivity half of Theorem~\ref{gts:vk:thm:sanov}, a classical theorem of Sanov~\cite{sanov}; source~08 plays ping-pong on the real projective line and Part~I on vectors of $\R^2$, so the proof above is a second proof (source~08). The second assertion, the free independence of distinct conjugates $E_j=Q^{-j}PQ^j$, is not in Part~I. Each $E_j$ has the congruence form of Part~I's image~\eqref{gts:vk:eq:San}, with chart coordinates $(j,1,-4j^2,-j)$. In this Part the letters $A_i$, $B_i$, $C$ name $4\times4$ generators, never Part~I's shears (Table~\ref{gts:um:tab:letters}).

Assign the letters of $\Sigma$ in their displayed order to $E_1,\ldots,E_{20}$, and $\#$ to $E_{21}$. Let $\Phi$ multiply these matrices in word order. Lemma~\ref{gts:um:lem:free} makes $\Phi$ injective even on the free group, hence on positive words. In a separate block put $t=E_0=P$ and $x_i=E_i$ for $1\leq i\leq114$.

\subsection{All generators and all target entries are numerical}
For the already fixed 114 tiles define
\begin{align}
 A_i&=\diag\bigl(\Phi(h_i),x_i\bigr),\label{gts:um:eq:A}\\
 B_i&=\diag\bigl(\Phi(g_i)^{-1},t^{-1}x_i^{-1}t\bigr),\label{gts:um:eq:B}\\
 C&=\diag\bigl(\Phi(X\#)^{-1},t\bigr).\label{gts:um:eq:C}
\end{align}
The inverse in \eqref{gts:um:eq:B} is the inverse of the whole product. For any determinant-one block,
\[
 \smallmat{a}{b}{c}{d}^{-1}=\smallmat{d}{-b}{-c}{a}.
\]
Thus the generator list contains only explicit integers, with no unspecified presentations, existentially chosen matrices, or input-dependent entries. For example,
\[
 A_1=\begin{pmatrix}5&2&0&0\\-8&-3&0&0\\0&0&5&2\\0&0&-8&-3\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 C=\begin{pmatrix}157&4&0&0\\-6712&-171&0&0\\0&0&1&2\\0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
The lower free-group words $x_i$, $t^{-1}x_j^{-1}t$, and $t$ are pairwise distinct. Hence all $2\cdot114+1=229$ generators are distinct even if some upper blocks coincide. They lie in $\SL_4(\Z)$ because both blocks have determinant one.

For any query word $w\in\Sigma^*$ define
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:target}
 \mathcal T(w)=\diag\bigl(\Phi(w\#)^{-1},t\bigr),\qquad
 E(\ell,\rho)=\mathcal T(w(\ell,\rho)).
\end{equation}
This computes the target without simulating $U$ or deciding membership.

\section[The exact paired form of unrestricted semigroup words]{Why unrestricted semigroup words have the exact paired form}
\label{gts:um:sec:marker}
The correspondence equation is constrained, whereas membership in $S$ quantifies over arbitrary positive generator words. The next lemma is the bridge. It rules out spurious orders at every length, rather than checking a bounded sample.

\begin{lemma}[Marker normal form]\label{gts:um:lem:marker}
A positive word in $\mathcal G$ has lower block $t$ if and only if it is
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:normal}
 A_{i_1}\cdots A_{i_r}\,C\,B_{i_r}\cdots B_{i_1}
\end{equation}
for some $r\geq0$ and indices $i_s\in\{1,\ldots,114\}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Work in the free group on $t,x_1,\ldots,x_{114}$, faithfully represented by the lower blocks. The exponent homomorphism $\eta$ defined by $\eta(t)=1$, $\eta(x_i)=0$ sends each $A_i,B_i$ lower block to zero and $C$ to one. Since the word is positive, a value equal to $t$ forces exactly one occurrence of $C$.

The kernel of $\eta$ has free basis
\[
 y_{i,h}=t^h x_i t^{-h},\qquad 1\leq i\leq114,\quad h\in\Z.
\]
Here is a direct justification. In a reduced word in these symbols, group consecutive letters of equal height $h$ into maximal runs. The $x$ word within a run remains nonempty and reduced; consecutive runs are separated on expansion by a nonzero power of $t$. The expansion is therefore nontrivial in the free $t,x$ group. Conversely, moving every $t$ letter rightward and recording the height at each $x$ letter expresses every total-height-zero word in the displayed generators.

Write the lower product as $Kt$, pushing the final $t$ to the right. Before the unique $C$, the height is zero; after it, the height is one. The contributions to $K$ are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}\toprule
Position & Generator & Contribution\\\midrule
before $C$ & $A_i$ & $y_{i,0}$\\
before $C$ & $B_i$ & $y_{i,-1}^{-1}$\\
after $C$ & $A_i$ & $y_{i,1}$\\
after $C$ & $B_i$ & $y_{i,0}^{-1}$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Equality with $t$ means $K=1$. Every height $-1$ letter occurs only negatively, so no inverse occurrence can cancel it; every height $+1$ letter occurs only positively. No such letter can disappear in free reduction. Thus no $B$ precedes $C$, and no $A$ follows $C$. The remaining kernel word is a positive word in the $y_{i,0}$ followed by a negative word. It cancels exactly when the latter indices reverse the former. This gives \eqref{gts:um:eq:normal}. Conversely, a word of that form cancels directly to lower block $t$.
\end{proof}

The exponent used here is the $t$ exponent in the free basis $t,x_1,\ldots,x_{114}$, not the $P$ exponent in the ambient $P,Q$ group. No cyclic rotation, chosen start marker, inverse-generator closure, or external regular-language restriction is part of the argument.

\section{The complete membership reduction}
\label{gts:um:sec:reduction}
For a normal-form word with sequence $s=i_1\cdots i_r$, its upper block is
\[
 \Phi(h(s))\,\Phi(X\#)^{-1}\,\Phi(g(s))^{-1}.
\]
Indeed the reversed $B$ order is
$\Phi(g_{i_r})^{-1}\cdots\Phi(g_{i_1})^{-1}
=\Phi(g(s))^{-1}$. Equality with the upper block of $\mathcal T(w)$ is equivalent, multiplying on the left by $\Phi(w\#)$ and on the right by $\Phi(g(s))\Phi(X\#)$, to
\[
 \Phi(w\#h(s))=\Phi(g(s)X\#).
\]
Both arguments are positive words, so injectivity gives precisely the literal equation of Lemma~\ref{gts:um:lem:tiles}. Combining that lemma with the marker normal form proves
\[
 \mathcal T(w)\in S\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad w\st X.
\]
For $w=X$, the empty inner sequence is realized by the nonempty generator product $C$. No missing identity convention is required. Main-interface inputs in \eqref{gts:um:eq:initial} contain state $A$ and are never the word $X$.

Applying Lemma~\ref{gts:um:lem:rewrite} proves the executable equivalence in Theorem~\ref{gts:um:thm:semigroup}. Section~\ref{gts:um:sec:machine} supplies a computable many-one reduction from ordinary halting to the finite-tape source language, and the implemented target map composes with it. Finally enumerate all nonempty words in the 229 generators, compute their exact integer products, and compare them with the query. This semidecides membership. Hence the fixed semigroup membership language is r.e. and many-one hard for every r.e. language, proving its many-one r.e.-completeness.

This mechanism is situated within the matrix undecidability literature, including Halava--Harju--Hirvensalo's correspondence-based constructions \cite{um-HHH}. The present proof does not import an optimized Claus-instance generator count. Its 229-generator figure is a verified count of this specified construction, not a bound claimed to improve the literature.

\wtagc\ The problem is positive-semigroup membership: no inverse generator is adjoined, and Lemma~\ref{gts:um:lem:marker} uses positivity (a lower block $t$ forces exactly one occurrence of $C$). It differs from the subgroup membership of Part~IV's fixed subgroups of $\SL_4(\Z)$ (Theorem~\ref{gts:ai:thm:quadratic}, Proposition~\ref{gts:ai:prop:uniform}) and of Part~VI of~\cite{pqc}; neither kind of result implies the other. This write did not survey the matrix-semigroup literature, and no priority is certified.

\section{A literal quartic certificate for each numerical length}
\label{gts:um:sec:polynomial}
Let $U_i$ be the upper block of $A_i$, and let $V_i$ be the inverse of the entire upper block of $B_i$. Then $U_i=\Phi(h_i)$ and $V_i=\Phi(g_i)$, all fixed integral determinant-one matrices. Keep $D$ from \eqref{gts:um:eq:DP}. A normal-form product has upper block
\[
 H_r D G_r^{-1},\qquad H_r=U_{i_1}\cdots U_{i_r},\quad
 G_r=V_{i_1}\cdots V_{i_r}.
\]
Its equality to $T$ can therefore be tested without auxiliary inverse entries by $H_rD=TG_r$.

\subsection{Every variable is allocated}
For every $s=1,\ldots,r$, allocate the following natural variables:
\begin{itemize}
\item 114 selectors $e_{s,i}$, $1\leq i\leq114$
\item 16 signed-part variables $h^+_{s,a,b},h^-_{s,a,b},g^+_{s,a,b},g^-_{s,a,b}$, $a,b\in\{0,1\}$
\end{itemize}
There are exactly $130r$ natural auxiliary slots. Define the linear expressions
\[
 H_{s,a,b}=h^+_{s,a,b}-h^-_{s,a,b},\qquad
 G_{s,a,b}=g^+_{s,a,b}-g^-_{s,a,b}.
\]
These expressions are abbreviations, not additional variables. Set $H_0=G_0=I_2$ as literal constants. The four $t_{ab}$ are signed external parameters, not quantified auxiliaries.

\subsection{Every residual is specified}
There is one selection residual per step:
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:select}
 S_s=\sum_{i=1}^{114}e_{s,i}-1.
\end{equation}
There are eight update residuals per step:
\begin{align}
 U^H_{s,a,b}&=H_{s,a,b}-\sum_{i=1}^{114}e_{s,i}
                  \sum_{k=0}^1 H_{s-1,a,k}(U_i)_{k,b},\label{gts:um:eq:updateH}\\
 U^G_{s,a,b}&=G_{s,a,b}-\sum_{i=1}^{114}e_{s,i}
                  \sum_{k=0}^1 G_{s-1,a,k}(V_i)_{k,b}.\label{gts:um:eq:updateG}
\end{align}
There are eight canonicality residuals per step:
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:complement}
 K^H_{s,a,b}=h^+_{s,a,b}h^-_{s,a,b},\qquad
 K^G_{s,a,b}=g^+_{s,a,b}g^-_{s,a,b}.
\end{equation}
Finally there are four terminal residuals:
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:terminal}
 L_{a,b}=\sum_{k=0}^1H_{r,a,k}D_{k,b}
                 -\sum_{k=0}^1t_{a,k}G_{r,k,b}.
\end{equation}
The literal polynomial is
\begin{align}\label{gts:um:eq:SOS}
 F_r(T;x)={}&\sum_{s=1}^r\left[S_s^2+
 \sum_{a,b=0}^1\left((U^H_{s,a,b})^2+(U^G_{s,a,b})^2
 +(K^H_{s,a,b})^2+(K^G_{s,a,b})^2\right)\right]\notag\\
 &+\sum_{a,b=0}^1L_{a,b}^2.
\end{align}
There are $(1+8+8)r+4=17r+4$ squared residuals. The natural domain alone makes the selectors one-hot; separate Booleanity equations would be redundant and are not counted. There are no hidden arithmetic-gate, determinant, quotient, inverse, or exponentiation witnesses.

\section{Soundness completeness and the precise multiplicity theorem}
\label{gts:um:sec:bijection}
\begin{lemma}[Unique canonical signed parts]\label{gts:um:lem:canonical}
For each $z\in\Z$ there is exactly one $(p,n)\in\N^2$ with $p-n=z$ and $pn=0$, namely $(\max(z,0),\max(-z,0))$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $z>0$, then $p=n+z>0$, so $pn=0$ forces $n=0$, $p=z$. If $z<0$, interchange the roles. If $z=0$, then $p=n$ and $p^2=0$, so both are zero. Each resulting pair satisfies the two equations.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{gts:um:thm:sos} apart from degree]
Since all values are integers, a sum of squares vanishes if and only if every residual vanishes. For a natural root, \eqref{gts:um:eq:select} says nonnegative integers sum to one, so exactly one selector $e_{s,i_s}$ equals one at each step and the others equal zero. The updates reduce to
\[
 H_s=H_{s-1}U_{i_s},\qquad G_s=G_{s-1}V_{i_s}.
\]
Starting at the constant identities, induction forces all signed prefix entries. The complement residuals and Lemma~\ref{gts:um:lem:canonical} then force their natural parts uniquely. The terminal residuals give $H_rD=TG_r$. Every $G_r$ is unimodular, so multiplication by $G_r^{-1}$ gives $T=H_rDG_r^{-1}$, exactly the upper block of \eqref{gts:um:eq:paired}. The lower block is $P$ by the marker lemma. This proves soundness.

Conversely, for any sequence satisfying \eqref{gts:um:eq:paired}, set its one-hot selectors, multiply the two prefix sequences, and use each entry's unique canonical signed parts. Selection, update, and complement residuals all vanish. Its target equality is $H_rD=TG_r$, so the terminal residuals vanish too. This proves completeness. The preceding forced induction proves that this is the only full auxiliary tuple for that specified sequence. Reading the unique selected index at each step is the inverse map. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:count}
 \#\{x\in\N^{130r}:F_r(T;x)=0\}
 =\#\{(i_1,\ldots,i_r):\eqref{gts:um:eq:paired}\}\leq114^r.
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

The assumption $x\in\N^{130r}$ is essential. Signed or real selectors summing to one are not automatically one-hot. The theorem does not silently replace this domain by $\Z$ or $\mathbb R$.

\subsection{Zero length and automatically excluded targets}
At $r=0$ the unique possible auxiliary tuple is empty. The formula becomes
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:r0}
 F_0(T)=(157-t_{00})^2+(4-t_{01})^2
              +(-6712-t_{10})^2+(-171-t_{11})^2.
\end{equation}
It has that single empty root precisely at $T=D$. Its positive generator witness is $C$, of length one, not the empty product.

Every satisfying $T$ has determinant one, since $T=H_rDG_r^{-1}$ is a product of determinant-one matrices. No determinant residual is needed. The mathematical external domain is nevertheless all of $\Z^4$, including zero and negative entries; determinant-invalid targets simply have empty fibers. For a fixed sequence, changing $T$ cannot preserve the terminal equations because $G_r$ is invertible. A changed target might of course be realized by another sequence.

The implementation rejects floats, booleans, malformed matrix shapes, negative auxiliary values, and missing or extra variable names. Its full-$4\times4$ helper accepts only inputs of the form $\diag(T,P)$. A rejection for wrong off-diagonal or lower blocks means the input lies outside this helper's interface; it is not a theorem of nonmembership in the original semigroup.

\subsection{Optional all-natural external inputs}
An independently charged variant uses eight external natural parameters $t^+_{ab},t^-_{ab}$, replaces $t_{ab}$ by their difference, and adds the four squared residuals $(t^+_{ab}t^-_{ab})^2$. It still uses exactly $130r$ quantified auxiliaries and now has $17r+8$ residuals. Canonical external pairs represent signed targets uniquely. Noncanonical pairs have a positive added square and no roots even if their differences are otherwise valid. External slots remain parameters; they do not increase the quantified auxiliary count.

\section{Degrees coefficients and literal sparse size}
\label{gts:um:sec:size}
The polynomial exporter lists every residual as integer-coefficient monomials and declares their sum of squares. This is an explicit polynomial representation: no numerical oracle or unevaluated matrix-word coefficient remains. Fully expanding the outer squares is unnecessary. The release includes both external modes at $r=0,1,2$ with all names and coefficients materialized.

\subsection{Exact degree and fixed coefficient data}
At $s=1$ the update equations are linear because $H_0=G_0=I_2$. Later updates are bilinear in selectors and previous state parts. Complements are quadratic; terminal residuals are bilinear for $r\geq1$ and linear at $r=0$. Thus $\deg F_r\leq4$. For $r\geq1$, a state complement has a nonzero quadratic homogeneous part. The degree-four component of a sum of real polynomial squares is a sum of squares of the quadratic leading parts; it cannot vanish identically when one of those parts is nonzero. Therefore $\deg F_r=4$. Equation~\eqref{gts:um:eq:r0} gives degree two at $r=0$. In the all-natural external variant, the external complements supply degree four even at $r=0$.

The independently audited literal source has 456 nonzero entries among the $U_i$ and 456 among the $V_i$. Their maximum absolute values are respectively
\[
 63{,}038{,}000\quad\text{and}\quad11{,}924{,}776.
\]
The maximum absolute $D$ entry is $6{,}712$. Hence the largest residual coefficient for $r\geq1$ is exactly $63{,}038{,}000$, with 26 magnitude bits; at $r=0$ it is $6{,}712$, with 13 magnitude bits. No coefficient-size contribution is concealed by the shorthand $U_i,V_i$.

\subsection{Monomial census with a declared convention}
Let $N_d$ count degree-$d$ monomial occurrences across the residuals, combining equal monomials within each residual but not across different residual squares. Let $R$ be the number of residuals. For signed external targets,
\begin{align}
 r=0:&\qquad (N_0,N_1,N_2)=(4,4,0),\quad R=4,\label{gts:um:eq:mono0}\\
 r\geq1:&\qquad (N_0,N_1,N_2)
     =(r,130r+928,3656r-3632),\quad R=17r+4.\label{gts:um:eq:monor}
\end{align}
Here is the complete accounting. Each of the $r$ selector residuals contributes one constant and 114 linear terms. Each of the eight first-step updates has 116 linear terms: two signed parts and 114 selected fixed coefficients. Each of the $8(r-1)$ later updates has two linear and 456 quadratic terms, because each of 114 selectors multiplies two matrix entries, each represented by two signed parts. The $8r$ complements each contribute one quadratic monomial. Each of four terminal residuals contributes four linear and four quadratic terms. Consequently
\[
 N_1=114r+8\cdot116+16(r-1)+16=130r+928,
\]
\[
 N_2=8\cdot456(r-1)+8r+16=3656r-3632.
\]
All stated coefficients are nonzero, and distinct indices separate the counted monomials. Total residual size is therefore $3787r-2704$ monomials for $r\geq1$.

For natural external targets the triples are $(4,8,4)$ at $r=0$, and
\[
 (N_0,N_1,N_2)=(r,130r+928,3656r-3612)\quad(r\geq1).
\]
The increase is 16 quadratic terms from splitting the signed terminal inputs and four external complement terms. Here $R=17r+8$, and the total for $r\geq1$ is $3787r-2684$.

No residual has more than 458 monomials. If the SOS is fully expanded, a safe coefficient bound for either mode and every $r$ is
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:coefficient}
 R\bigl(458\cdot63{,}038{,}000\bigr)^2.
\end{equation}
Indeed each residual's coefficient $\ell^1$ norm is at most the quantity in parentheses, the norm is submultiplicative under squaring, and the $R$ squares add. This conservative bound has bit length $O(\log(r+1))$. It is not a claim that the expanded polynomial is an efficient storage format.

\section{Resource ledgers with arithmetic and bit costs separated}
\label{gts:um:sec:resources}
\subsection{Fixed matrices and finite-tape target encoding}
The numerical source census is
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lr}\toprule
Object & Exact count\\\midrule
Machine states and tape symbols & $15$ and $2$\\
Defined transitions & $29$\\
Rewrite alphabet letters and directed rules & $20$ and $93$\\
Copy tiles, rewrite tiles, separator tiles & $20$, $93$, $1$\\
Inner tiles and distinct $4\times4$ generators & $114$ and $229$\\
All matrix-entry slots & $3664$\\
Nonzero matrix entries & $1831$\\
Sum of matrix-entry magnitude bit lengths & $21372$\\
Largest absolute generator entry & $63038000$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Magnitude bit length means $\operatorname{bit\_length}(|z|)$, with zero counted as zero. The 21,372 total is a census of mathematical values, not a file-size or compression theorem.

Let $n=|\ell|+|\rho|$. Then $w$ has $n+4$ letters, and $w\#$ has $n+5$. The target encoder makes $n+5$ exact $2\times2$ multiplications by fixed matrices. Since
\[
 \max_{1\leq j\leq21}\umnorm{E_j}=3611<2^{12},
\]
every target entry has at most $12(n+5)+1$ magnitude bits, a deliberately conservative bound also covering the fixed lower block. The inverse formula changes two signs and swaps the diagonal entries once. The implementation additionally verifies determinant one with two variable-integer multiplications and one subtraction.

Each fixed-matrix multiplication uses eight scalar fixed-integer multiplications and four additions. The $k$th prefix contains $O(k)$ bits, so elementary fixed-integer arithmetic costs $O(k)$; summing gives $O(n^2)$ bit operations. The final determinant check also costs at most $O(n^2)$ with schoolbook multiplication. Working values use $O(n)$ bits, apart from stored input/output. This is complexity in the supplied finite tape length, not arbitrary source-program length or halting time. Far-end zero padding can change the finite word and target while describing the same infinite tape; the reduction holds for either spelling and needs no semantic-tape injectivity.

\subsection{Exact generic sparse evaluation charges}
The companion evaluator deliberately uses an unoptimized convention. For each monomial it starts at 1, multiplies once for every variable occurrence, multiplies by its coefficient even when that coefficient is $1$ or $-1$, and adds the result into an initially zero residual accumulator. It then squares every residual and adds the square into an initially zero SOS accumulator. Substituting \eqref{gts:um:eq:mono0}--\eqref{gts:um:eq:monor} gives
\[
 M=N_1+2N_2+(N_0+N_1+N_2)+R,\qquad
 A=N_0+N_1+N_2+R.
\]
Thus the exact signed-interface census is
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lrr}\toprule
Numerical length & Scalar multiplications $M$ & Scalar additions $A$\\\midrule
$r=0$ & $16$ & $12$\\
$r\geq1$ & $11246r-9036$ & $3804r-2700$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Natural external inputs cost 40 multiplications and 24 additions at $r=0$; for $r\geq1$, add 64 multiplications and 24 additions to the signed figures. These counts belong to this precise evaluator, not to an optimal arithmetic circuit. They exclude parsing, comparisons, variable lookup, hashing, validation, loop control, source loading, and polynomial construction. Those operations remain finite implementation work and are separately bounded or documented; they are not free witnesses hidden in the polynomial.

The fixed loader calls its determinant-one $2\times2$ inverse routine 572 times: 229 upper blocks, 229 lower blocks, and 114 extracted $V_i$ blocks. Its arithmetic is 1,144 multiplications, 572 subtractions, and 1,144 negations, apart from parsing, hashing, shape/domain checks, swaps, and control. The literal coefficient JSON has 117,288 bytes. None of these arithmetic counts is the matrix-generator count, and no earlier constant-operation claim is inherited.

\subsection{Certificate construction and value growth}
Direct auditing gives
\[
 K_H=\max_i\umnorm{U_i}=64{,}675{,}047<2^{26},\qquad
 K_G=\max_i\umnorm{V_i}=12{,}234{,}453<2^{24}.
\]
The maximum absolute row-sum norm is submultiplicative, so at prefix $s\geq1$,
\[
 \umnorm{H_s}\leq K_H^s<2^{26s},\qquad
 \umnorm{G_s}\leq K_G^s<2^{24s}.
\]
Each canonical $H$ part needs at most $26s$ magnitude bits and each $G$ part at most $24s$. Only one part of each pair is nonzero. Four entries of each kind plus the one selected bit per step give the total bound
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:certbits}
 \sum_{s=1}^r(4\cdot26s+4\cdot24s+1)
 =100r(r+1)+r
\end{equation}
on auxiliary magnitude bits, counting zero as zero. A storage convention assigning at least one bit to every slot adds $O(130r)$; names and explicit zero slots further affect text size.

Since $\umnorm D=6883<2^{13}$ and for determinant-one $2\times2$ matrices
$\umnorm{G^{-1}}\leq2\umnorm G$, any realized target entry has at most $50r+14$ magnitude bits. This upper bound is not a claim that every sequence achieves exponential growth.

Constructing the two prefixes takes two fixed-factor $2\times2$ multiplications per step, hence $16r$ scalar multiplications and $8r$ additions, plus comparisons, canonical splitting, and selector output. Streaming prefixes uses $O(r)$ working bits and $O(r^2)$ elementary bit operations. Retaining the entire certificate uses $O(r^2)$ value bits; explicit indexed names add $O(r\log(r+1))$ textual overhead. A supplied target adds its encoded length to reading/checking cost. If the target is computed from the sequence, two terminal $2\times2$ products add 16 multiplications and eight additions, and the determinant-one inverse check adds two multiplications, one subtraction, two negations, and entry swaps. The final nonconstant product costs $O(r^2)$ schoolbook bit operations. This work is not included in the polynomial's auxiliary count.

\subsection{Evaluating arbitrary assignments and constructing the family}
Suppose every signed external entry and natural auxiliary entry has absolute value less than $2^B$, $B\geq1$. Each residual has at most 458 monomials, degree at most two, and coefficient magnitude less than $2^{26}$. Therefore each residual has magnitude less than $2^{2B+35}$. The SOS has magnitude-bit length at most
\[
 4B+70+\lceil\log_2R\rceil.
\]
The evaluator has $O(r+1)$ counted arithmetic operations, on integers of $O(B+\log(r+1))$ bits, and hence the conservative schoolbook upper bound
\[
 O\bigl((r+1)(B+\log(r+1))^2\bigr)
\]
bit operations. Variable/domain validation and parsing add their ordinary finite costs.

A streaming residual generator need not store the expanded SOS. The compiler emits $O(r+1)$ residual monomials with the explicit constants of Section~\ref{gts:um:sec:size}, and $O((r+1)\log(r+2))$ name characters. Its work is linear in the numerical value of $r$ at the symbolic output level, not polynomial in the binary encoding length of $r$. This output-sensitive accounting is essential when a family index is passed as an integer.

\section{Two concrete examples and what their certificates mean}
\label{gts:um:sec:examples}
\subsection{An actual accepting finite tape}
The accepted input $\ell=011$, $\rho=\eps$ has initial word $[110A0]$ and target
\[
 E(011,\eps)=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 1347733333&37956152&0&0\\
 -57391245432&-1616307011&0&0\\
 0&0&1&2\\0&0&0&1
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Its full derivation is
\begin{align*}
 [110A0]&\hto[1100B0]\hto[11001C0]\hto[1100G10]\\
 &\hto[110G010]\hto[11H0010]\hto[1I11010]\hto[J111010]\\
 &\hto[X11010]\hto[X1010]\hto[X010]\hto[X10]\\
 &\hto[X0]\hto[X]\hto X.
\end{align*}
The first seven steps are machine transitions, the eighth is the halting rewrite, and the remaining six erase the finite tape and finish. The tile compiler encodes these 14 rewrites using 94 tiles. The corresponding $A$--$C$--$B$ word has $2\cdot94+1=189$ generator factors, whose generic integer $4\times4$ product equals the displayed target in all sixteen entries.

The signed-target certificate at $r=94$ has
\[
 130r=12{,}220\quad\text{natural auxiliaries},\qquad
 17r+4=1{,}602\quad\text{residuals}.
\]
The saved assignment has SOS value exactly zero, largest auxiliary magnitude 409 bits, and total auxiliary magnitude-bit census 154,084. The generic evaluator charges are exactly 1,048,088 multiplications and 354,876 additions. The complete assignment is included as \code{paired/examples/accepting-94-certificate.json}; no huge expanded quartic is needed to verify it.

\subsection{A length two target with two distinct roots}
The distinct sequences $(20,109)$ and $(110,20)$ both give
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:collision}
 T_*=
 \smallmat{22474169}{568874}{-892774960}{-22598231}.
\end{equation}
The mechanism is visible at the word level. Tile 20 is $(X,X)$, tile 109 is $(X,0X)$, and tile 110 is $(X,X0)$. Both sequences therefore have
\[
 h(s)=XX,\qquad g(s)=X0X.
\]
They give identical $H_2,G_2$ and thus the same target $H_2DG_2^{-1}$, but their selector choices, first prefixes, and complete auxiliary tuples differ. Each tuple has 260 natural slots and makes all 38 residuals vanish. The saved \code{paired/examples/target-collision.json} binds these sequences to the literal numerical target.

An exhaustive enumeration of the $114^2=12{,}996$ length-two sequences found 12,994 distinct targets and two excess sequence collisions. This is a finite diagnostic, not a classification of all multiplicities at greater lengths. Equation~\eqref{gts:um:eq:count} concerns the number of sequence witnesses at one fixed $r$; it does not imply a unique target root or a finite number of roots after allowing all lengths.

\wtagc\ The second excess collision is $(20,111)$ and $(112,20)$, by the same mechanism with the bit~$1$: tile~111 is $(X,1X)$ and tile~112 is $(X,X1)$, so both sequences have $h(s)=XX$ and $g(s)=X1X$. Source~08 names only the first. Both were found by an independent enumeration of the 12,996 products from the literal generators at placement, and again at this write from the shipped \path{data/08-matrix-semigroup-core-data-semigroup.json}.

\section{Exact factorization fibers and their asymptotic growth}
\label{gts:um:sec:fibers}
The bounded-family theorem can be sharpened substantially for targets that encode actual accepted configurations. This section counts \emph{factorization words}, equivalently tile sequences or canonical polynomial roots, not distinct matrices. All the words counted in a fiber have the same resulting matrix.

For $w\in\Sigma^*$, set $T_w=\Phi(w\#)^{-1}$ and define
\[
 N_w(r)=\#\{s:|s|=r,\ w\#h(s)=g(s)X\#\}.
\]
By the marker lemma and the certificate bijection, this is simultaneously the number of generator words for $\diag(T_w,P)$ of length $2r+1$ and the number of roots of $F_r(T_w;x)$.

\subsection{Every accepted encoded word has an infinite unbounded fiber}
Write $c(v\#)$ for the copy tiles of $v$ followed by tile 114. Both its tile-side words equal $v\#$. If $s$ is a witness for $w$, then
\[
 s_k=c(w\#)^k s\qquad(k\geq0)
\]
is another witness: both sides of the original word equality acquire the same prefix $(w\#)^k$. Its length is $|s|+k(|w|+1)$. Thus every represented encoded word target has infinitely many distinct factorization words at unbounded lengths, and infinitely many canonical roots in the disjoint union over $r$. Every fixed-$r$ fiber remains finite. This is a proved obstruction to unbounded finite-foldness of this particular family, rather than merely a missing finite-fold proof.

One may also append any number of $c(X\#)$ blocks. For the special terminal input $w=X$, these are exactly all witnesses, so
\[
 \sum_{r\geq0}N_X(r)z^r=\frac1{1-z^2}.
\]
The empty inner word at $r=0$ represents the one-generator word $C$.

\subsection{An exact rational generating function for every halting live input}
Suppose $w$ is a live configuration that halts. Let its deterministic machine run make $H$ moves before reaching the undefined cell. Write its live words as
\[
 v_0=w,\ v_1,\ldots,v_H=[\,l\,J\,1\,v\,],\qquad
 n_j=|v_j|,\qquad m=|l|+|v|,\qquad B=\binom{m}{|l|}.
\]
Here $H$ excludes the halting rewrite and all cleanup. Define
\begin{align}
 r_*&=\sum_{j=0}^{H-1}(n_j-1)+(m+4)
              +\sum_{a=1}^{m}(a+3)+2,\label{gts:um:eq:rstar}\\
 d&=H+m+2.\label{gts:um:eq:d}
\end{align}
Empty sums are zero. There are $d$ genuine rewrites from $w$ through cleanup to $X$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact accepted-input fiber]\label{gts:um:thm:GF}
For a halting live input as above,
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:GF}
 \sum_{r\geq0}N_w(r)z^r
 =\frac{Bz^{r_*}}
 {\displaystyle\left(\prod_{j=0}^{H}(1-z^{n_j+1})\right)
  \left(\prod_{q=4}^{m+4}(1-z^q)\right)(1-z^2)}.
\end{equation}
Repeated weights are retained. There are $d+1$ denominator factors.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Separator parsing from Lemma~\ref{gts:um:lem:tiles} uniquely splits every witness into complete separator-terminated blocks, without a trailing nonseparator suffix. Each rule left side contains exactly one state or $X$. Every reachable word contains exactly one such marker. Since a block partitions its bottom word into disjoint tile sides, it can contain at most one rule tile.

A block with no rule tile is exactly the unique all-copy block $c(v\#)$ for its current word. A block with one rule tile is exactly the copies of its left context, that rule tile, its right context copies, and a separator. Thus every witness has a uniquely determined genuine rewrite history interspersed with uniquely counted copy loops.

Before the halting rewrite, the history is the deterministic machine run. It has no repeated live word, since such a repetition would force a deterministic cycle and prevent halting. After $J1\hto X$, the word is $[lXv]$. Cleanup removes the rightmost remaining left symbol or the leftmost remaining right symbol, then performs $[X]\hto X$. The $m$ erasure schedules are exactly the $B$ choices of the $|l|$ left deletions among $m$ positions. They yield distinct sequences of rule blocks even when some tape bits coincide, because left and right erasure rules are distinct. Later merging of intermediate words does not identify those block sequences.

For a rule $u\hto u'$ in a source word of length $n$, its block uses $n-|u|$ context-copy tiles, one rule tile, and one separator, hence $n-|u|+2$ tiles. Machine rules have $|u|=3$, giving costs $n_j-1$. The halt rule has $|u|=2$ in a word of length $m+4$, giving cost $m+4$. The tape erasures have source lengths $m+3,m+2,\ldots,4$ and $|u|=2$, hence those same costs. The final three-letter rule costs two. Summing gives the common genuine-history cost $r_*$ in \eqref{gts:um:eq:rstar}.

At any visited word of length $n$, one copy loop costs $n+1$, and any number of such loops is possible. The live words give weights $n_0+1,\ldots,n_H+1$. The cleanup words after the halt have lengths $m+3,m+2,\ldots,3,1$, giving weights $m+4,m+3,\ldots,4,2$. Every one of the $B$ genuine histories has exactly this same multiset of weights.

Copy-loop multiplicities at each history position are independent. Separator parsing recovers the rule blocks and all intervening loop counts, so different choices never collide. Multiplying the geometric series for the loop counts and summing over the $B$ disjoint histories yields \eqref{gts:um:eq:GF}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Support quasipolynomials and inversion}
Let $a_1,\ldots,a_{d+1}$ be the denominator weights of \eqref{gts:um:eq:GF}, and put
\[
 A=\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}a_i,\qquad L=\operatorname{lcm}(a_1,\ldots,a_{d+1}).
\]
Every such weight list contains 2, 4, and 5. Weight 2 occurs at $X$, weight 4 at $[X]$, and weight 5 occurs at a length-four cleanup word if $m\geq1$, or at the halt word if $m=0$. No reachable word has length zero or two, so there are no loop weights 1 or 3. Nonnegative combinations of 2 and 5 are exactly $\{0,2\}\cup\{n:n\geq4\}$, and every other weight belongs to that same set. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:support}
 N_w(r)>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 r\in\{r_*,r_*+2\}\ \text{or}\ r\geq r_*+4.
\end{equation}
In particular $r_*$ is the minimum inner length and $N_w(r_*)=B$.

All poles of \eqref{gts:um:eq:GF} are roots of unity with orders dividing $L$. Moreover direct subtraction of \eqref{gts:um:eq:rstar} from the sum of the loop weights gives
\[
 \sum_i a_i-r_*=2H+m+5>0.
\]
Thus the rational function is proper. Partial fractions express its coefficients for every $r\geq0$ as sums of polynomials in $r$ times $L$th roots of unity to the $r$th power. Hence $N_w(r)$ is an exact quasipolynomial of period dividing $L$: on each residue class modulo $L$ it agrees with a polynomial. Properness means no extra polynomial-in-$z$ part is needed to correct the initial coefficient range.

Only $z=1$ has pole order $d+1$, since a root common to every denominator factor must have order dividing both 4 and 5. At $z=1$, the leading pole coefficient is $B/A$, using $(1-z^a)/(1-z)\to a$. All other poles have order at most $d$. Coefficient extraction gives
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:asymptotic}
 N_w(r)=\frac{B}{d!A}r^d+O(r^{d-1}).
\end{equation}
Every residue class has the same positive leading coefficient. The fixed accepted target therefore has polynomial, rather than exponential, factorization growth, with exponent depending on its halting history.

For the cumulative count and its generalized inverse,
\[
 C_w(R)=\sum_{0\leq r\leq R}N_w(r),\qquad
 R_w(M)=\min\{R\in\N:C_w(R)\geq M\},
\]
summation and monotone inversion give
\begin{align}
 C_w(R)&=\frac{B}{(d+1)!A}R^{d+1}+O(R^d),\label{gts:um:eq:cumulative}\\
 R_w(M)&=\left(\frac{(d+1)!A}{B}M\right)^{1/(d+1)}+O(1).
 \label{gts:um:eq:inverse}
\end{align}
For the inverse error, shifting the leading-order root by a sufficiently large constant changes the main cumulative term by a constant multiple of $R^d$, which bounds the remainder on both sides. Monotonicity then brackets the least admissible integer $R$. The generator-length threshold is $2R_w(M)+1$.

These are conditional formulas for an already accepted input. Obtaining $H$ and the live history requires reaching its halt; the formulas give no total decision procedure for whether an arbitrary finite input halts.

\subsection{Exact growth for the released 94 tile example}
For $w=[110A0]$, one has $H=7$, $m=5$, $|l|=0$, $B=1$, $d=14$, and $r_*=94$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{gts:um:eq:exampleGF}
 \sum_{r\geq0}N_w(r)z^r
 =\frac{z^{94}}
 {(1-z^2)(1-z^4)(1-z^5)(1-z^6)(1-z^7)
 (1-z^8)^2(1-z^9)^2(1-z^{10})^6}.
\end{equation}
Its weight product is $8{,}709{,}120{,}000{,}000$ and its period divides 2520. In particular
\[
 N_w(r)=\frac{r^{14}}{759{,}246{,}199{,}455{,}744{,}000{,}000{,}000}
                +O(r^{13}).
\]
The counts at $r=94,95,\ldots,106$ are
\[
 1,0,1,0,2,1,3,2,6,5,14,7,19.
\]
At $z=-1$ the pole order is 11, and every other nontrivial root of unity has pole order at most 7. Thus all periodic coefficient terms have degree at most ten: coefficients of degrees 14 through 11 in the quasipolynomial are independent of residue class. No complete list of its 2520 residue polynomials is claimed to have been tabulated.

The focused \code{fiber/} packet \cite{um-Fiber} contains the independent literal-tile graph checks and proof. Its checker reads numerical JSON and the saved witness without importing either compiler; finite stutter and coefficient tests support, but do not substitute for, the all-length arguments above.

\section{Evidence architecture and portable reproduction}
\label{gts:um:sec:reproduce}
\subsection{What has been checked and what each check establishes}
The local construction is proved above. Testing checks the implementation against independent arithmetic and bounded semantics; it is not a replacement for universality or an unbounded nonhalting argument. Normal Python and optimized \texttt{python -O} runs are both supplied, so correctness does not depend on removable assertions.

The core suite checks all 229 determinants and distinct generators, 6,750 local configuration contexts, all cleanup cases from 225 short binary contexts, and 64,036 matched machine/rewrite transitions over 225 bounded runs. Its exhaustive marker sample covers all 488,281 words of length at most eight over $A_1,A_2,B_1,B_2,C$; exactly 15 have lower block $P$, all in the proved form. It also verifies the actual 189-factor accepting word, 100 randomized target encodings, malformed inputs, the zero-step word $X$, and an 8,192-bit command-line input beyond Python's usual decimal conversion limit.

The companion suite checks six literal small-$r$ polynomial/count/degree cases, 162 zero-length target-cube cases, 228 length-one certificate evaluations, all 12,996 length-two products, 1,544 length-two certificate evaluations, 860 selector/state/target mutations, 18 invalid-input rejections, and determinant-invalid targets. It checks the complete long accepting certificate and its measured arithmetic charges. Separate audits read only the pinned literal numerical JSON rather than importing the upstream compiler, reconstruct the matrix formulas, inspect the interfaces, and instrument the arithmetic census.

The core mathematical review separately inspected the arbitrary-length marker proof, separator edge cases, upper-block order, finite-tape simulation, and published dependency. The companion mathematical review separately inspected domain restrictions, canonicality, bijection, degree, exact count formulas, invalid targets, and multiplicity. These are independent human-readable mathematical and programmatic reviews, not proof-assistant certificates.

The fiber checker enumerates 33,544 distinct bounded stutter witnesses and independently checks 12 full matrix/SOS samples. A separate literal-tile graph dynamic program compares 31,366 coefficients over 226 fixtures, with identical normal and optimized receipts. At the release level, an additional JSON-only audit reconstructs all six exported polynomials (11,970 residual monomials), 14 small roots, the full 94-step auxiliary assignment, and both collision roots without importing either compiler.

\paragraph{Evidence map.}
The following relative paths identify the principal review and receipt artifacts in the portable package. The release inventory binds their bytes; each review also states its mathematical scope.
\begin{itemize}
\item \code{core/audit/REVIEW.md} and \code{core/loader-audit/U15_DEPENDENCY_AUDIT.md}: local proof review and precise published input dependency
\item \code{core/evidence/tests.json}, \code{core/evidence/tests-optimized.json}, and \code{core/audit/independent-results.json}: machine, matrix, marker, and witness checks
\item \code{paired/audit/REVIEW.md} and \code{paired/audit/REVIEW_BINDING.json}: companion proof review and exact reviewed-file identities
\item \code{paired/evidence/tests.json}, \code{paired/evidence/tests-optimized.json}, and the JSON receipts in \code{paired/audit/}: authored and independent polynomial/interface checks
\item \code{fiber/PROOF.md}, \code{fiber/CHECKS.json}, and \code{fiber/audit/}: exact-fiber proof, stutter/sample checks, and independent graph-coefficient review
\item \code{verification/literal-polynomials.json} and \code{verification/expected-receipts.json}: independent release-level reconstruction and the strictly typed replay expectations
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Release contents and identity boundaries}
The portable release keeps the two source packets under \code{core/} and \code{paired/}, and the focused multiplicity analysis under \code{fiber/}, with authored code and numerical data retained byte-for-byte. Documentation whose original provenance contained machine-specific paths is accompanied by explicit sanitized provenance and fresh release identities. Earlier releases are unchanged. Third-party primary-PDF caches, interpreter caches, private machine paths, and transient compilation files are not part of this distribution. The bibliography links the primary sources instead.

The central numerical source is identical in both packets and has SHA-256
\begin{center}\small\ttfamily
506144b361b2bea634d468a94921644d91868\\
 dc089a257fe289a0bf51b74cec9
\end{center}
(the line break is typographical only). Its source accepting-witness JSON has SHA-256
\begin{center}\small\ttfamily
13a3857d28b0207d9baa83facac5b2e67bb\\
 aeb858d00b82ef9a91c4ab38df890
\end{center}
These content identities distinguish a replay of this numerical construction from a successful test of some edited construction. Checksums are integrity bindings, not mathematical proofs or digital signatures.

The release-level verifier first checks the complete expected path inventory and file identities before executing copied code. Scientific replay runs in fresh isolated copies, so build/test commands cannot overwrite the distributed evidence or original packets. Receipt comparisons validate exact key sets, values, and types; a boolean is not accepted in place of an integer merely because Python equates \code{True} and \code{1}. The normal and optimized modes have explicit mode fields. Release tests include tampered contents, inventory changes, unexpected paths, and receipt type mutations.

The executable instructions and exact current entry points are in \code{RELEASE_README.md}. From the release directory, the commands below check identity and then perform numerical replay:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
python3 -I -B verify\_release.py --verify-only\\
python3 -I -B verify\_release.py --replay
\end{quote}
 Save its redirected receipt outside the sealed release tree. \code{build_report.sh} rebuilds the PDF in an external output directory. The ordinary workflow is an integrity check, a complete replay, and optionally a PDF rebuild. The release records expected semantics as well as byte hashes: rebuilt matrix JSON, ledger, and literal list must match the pinned outputs; shared source JSON and witness identities must agree; saved normal/optimized and independent receipts must have the expected typed content. Relocation and extracted-archive replay exercise the same procedures without the original workspace.

\subsection{Rebuilding and reading the artifacts}
Only Python 3.10 or later and the standard library are required for numerical replay. A standard LaTeX installation with the packages listed at the start of \code{report32.tex} is needed to rebuild the PDF. The report source is an ordinary self-contained LaTeX file. The release README describes an isolated rebuild rather than overwriting the sealed report.

Useful direct scientific commands from the appropriate packet directory include
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
python compiler.py target --left 011 --right ''\\
python paired\_compiler.py ledger 94\\
python paired\_compiler.py export 1\\
python paired\_compiler.py verify examples/accepting-94-certificate.json
\end{quote}
The first command belongs to \code{core/}; the other three to \code{paired/}. A target command computes a matrix and is not a membership solver. Certificate verification checks a proposed bounded witness, not absence of witnesses. The polynomial exporter is explicit for any resource-feasible numerical $r$; actual execution remains subject to ordinary time and memory limits.

\wtagc\ \textbf{Shipped names and the Windows replay.} In this report the release is shipped flattened under the prefix \path{08-matrix-semigroup-}: programs in \path{code/}, numerical data and receipts in \path{data/}, and the proofs, reviews, packet READMEs, dependency audit and sanitized provenance at the report root; for example \path{core/compiler.py} is \path{code/08-matrix-semigroup-core-compiler.py} and \path{core/data/semigroup.json} is \path{data/08-matrix-semigroup-core-data-semigroup.json}. The README maps every name. Not shipped: \path{report32.tex} and \path{report32.pdf} (this Part is the article), \path{RELEASE_README.md}, the seals \path{MANIFEST.json}, \path{SHA256SUMS} and \path{fiber/SHA256SUMS}, the two $r=2$ exports (1.27~MB, which the shipped compiler regenerates byte for byte in under a second), twelve byte copies inside the release (among them the second and third copies of \path{semigroup.json} and of \path{accepting-witness.json}), and \path{core/data/u15_table.json}. The verifier's identity gate requires the complete delivered inventory, so \path{verify_release.py} cannot pass on the shipped files alone; the README gives a rerun from the arrival archive and a reconstruction of the delivered layout. As delivered, \texttt{verify\_release.py --replay} fails on Windows: \path{core/compiler.py}, \path{core/audit/independent_check.py}, \path{fiber/check_fibers.py}, \path{paired/tests/check_all.py} and \path{tamper_regression.py} write text with the platform newline, so the rebuilt \path{semigroup.json}, \path{ledger.json} and \path{matrices.txt} are CRLF files and the byte comparison stops the replay after about four seconds; after CRLF$\to$LF conversion they equal the delivered bytes. At placement every replay component was rerun on copies with LF output forced, in ordinary mode, and all but the deterministic export and certificate commands also in optimized mode; every receipt and rebuilt file matched; \path{tamper_regression.py} passed its 18 cases unmodified; the end-to-end replay was not rerun. The ``portable replay'' of this section is portable to POSIX systems. Several of these programs also overwrite recorded outputs next to themselves; run them only on copies.

\section{Consequences limitations and further questions}
\label{gts:um:sec:future}
The report separates three notions of finiteness. The generator list is fixed once and for all. A bounded certificate at numerical $r$ is a finite polynomial with a finite root set. The unbounded membership problem ranges over all $r$, and that union recovers r.e.-complete behavior. Confusing these levels would turn a correct bounded theorem into an unsupported uniform one.

The following questions are substantive next steps, not conclusions already obtained.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Materialize the upstream compiler.} Implement the published ordinary-machine to clockwise-machine to bi-tag to finite-$U$-tape transformation with explicit input conventions, proper finite boundary symbols, and checked halting correspondence. This would strengthen the executable endpoint without changing the mathematical dependency already sufficient for Theorem~\ref{gts:um:thm:semigroup}.
\item \textbf{Study target multiplicity.} Beyond the accepted-input fibers classified in Section~\ref{gts:um:sec:fibers}, characterize when different tile sequences have the same $(h(s),g(s))$, and when distinct such pairs still have the same $H_rDG_r^{-1}$. The explicit collision shows that canonical signed states alone cannot make target factorization unique. Any proposed canonical factorization selector needs its own soundness, completeness, and resource accounting.
\item \textbf{Reduce paid certificate size.} The one-hot selectors are transparent but account for 114 of 130 variables per step. Binary encodings or interpolation may trade variables against degree, coefficient height, and gate witnesses. A genuine improvement should give a full ledger in the same natural domain and preserve the exact root-sequence correspondence or state explicitly how it changes.
\item \textbf{Improve generator or dimension counts.} The local construction prioritizes a clean positive-word marker proof. The broader literature has other correspondence encodings. Any comparison should use the same fixed-versus-variable semigroup convention, coefficient domain, dimension, inverse policy, and input interface; the present count is not an optimality claim.

\wtagc\ (3~October 2026.) Question~4 is answered in part in the repository, on the day the archive arrived. The research programme's review~\cite{repo-revmg} reconstructed all 229 matrices and derived a subset of 197: delete the copy tiles of the fifteen state letters and of $X$, with their paired generators $A_i,B_i$. On a valid input every copied context consists of $0,1,[,]$, so the forward direction of Lemma~\ref{gts:um:lem:tiles} survives, and the subset semigroup has the same target map and the same halting equivalence on valid inputs, hence the same conditional r.e.-completeness; the all-word equivalence of Lemma~\ref{gts:um:lem:tiles} is not preserved. The successor note~\cite{repo-ds193} uses the terminal word $[J1]$ in place of $X$, with the four cleanup rules $0J1\hto J1$, $J10\hto J1$, $1J1\hto J1$, $J11\hto J1$ and the 87 machine rules (91 rewrites and 96 tiles), and obtains 193 literal generators in $\SL_4(\Z)$; the accepting example of Section~\ref{gts:um:sec:examples} then needs 12 rewrites, 83 tiles and 167 generator factors. Its largest entry grows from 26 to 31 magnitude bits, while the total of entry bit lengths falls from 21,372 to 19,321. Neither note claims minimality, an inverse-closed alphabet or a Diophantine gate bound, and both keep the Neary--Woods dependency. They are not printed here, and the shipped files are source~08's.
\item \textbf{Relate bounded search to source computation.} A particular machine derivation produces a tile witness by copying contexts. Section~\ref{gts:um:sec:fibers} gives the exact minimal tile length and growth for an accepted live input. Further bounds could express its formula in terms of source-machine time and space through the published upstream simulation, while charging any implemented input compiler separately.
\item \textbf{Formalize the proof interfaces.} A proof assistant could check the rewriting simulation, separator lemma, free-group marker lemma, and signed-part bijection, with data extraction tied to the source table. Existing exact arithmetic checks do not supply this formal derivation.
\wtagc\ The repository has no Lean or Rocq module for any statement of this Part.
\item \textbf{Investigate a uniform unbounded polynomial.} Any encoding of all lengths into one fixed-arity equation must explicitly pay for sequences, arithmetic, and canonicality. The present family is not such a construction. The explicit stuttering theorem proves that this family has infinite unbounded fibers for every accepted encoded target. A different uniform representation would need a separate multiplicity analysis.
\wtagc\ This is the part of Part~IV's question~10 (Section~\ref{gts:ai:sec:questions}) that this Part leaves open; the note there records what Part~V supplies.
\wtagd\ (4~October 2026.) Answered in part by Part~\ref{gts:part:cm}, for a successor of this Part's semigroup and one pair of fixed contexts. Its Theorem~\ref{gts:cm:thm:main} is one positive-integer polynomial of fixed arity, with 41,309 witnesses and exact degree~12, whose zeros at an ordinary input encode accepting histories of every length, with sequences and arithmetic paid explicitly. It does not pay for canonicality: offsets and congruence quotients vary, it states no finite-fold or unique-witness representation (Section~\ref{gts:cm:sec:equivalence}), and it gives no multiplicity analysis. Its semigroup is the research programme's 195-generator singular $7\times7$ successor, reached through the 193-generator semigroup of question~4, not the 229 matrices of this Part. The programme's uniform compilers of the same night do the same uniformly over program recipes, at much lower cost and much higher degree (note in Section~\ref{gts:cm:sec:scope}).
\end{enumerate}

\section{Conclusion}
One literal positive semigroup in $\SL_4(\Z)$ supports an exact finite-tape halting reduction. Its lower free-group block enforces the required paired factors inside ordinary unrestricted semigroup membership, while its upper block faithfully encodes a directed correspondence equation. The published effective universal-machine construction supplies the only nonmaterialized reduction stage needed for the many-one r.e.-completeness conclusion.

At every numerical inner length, the companion polynomial gives a fully charged quartic natural-witness interface with $130r$ auxiliaries and $17r+4$ residuals. Its roots encode sequences canonically, not targets uniquely. For accepted inputs the exact rational fiber formula also determines minimal length, all supported lengths, quasipolynomial multiplicities, and polynomial asymptotic growth. The numerical examples, arithmetic ledgers, and identity-gated replay make these results and their limits inspectable.

Its author line reads ``Report55''; it names no author and is AI-assisted. Its abstract and reading guide follow.

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Abstract (source 09).} We give a fully expanded integer polynomial that recognizes finite accepting paths of the actual pinned five-register matrix countdown, with its ordinary positive input $x$ unchanged. A single common offset encodes signed row coordinates; explicitly paid binary-containment clauses select one of 97 synchronized branches at every time; exact shift equalities enforce the initial frame, every internal join, and the last frame. A fixed coefficient bound makes the signed affine updates carry-free. The counter forces exactly $x$ initial loader steps followed by a tile suffix.

The polynomial has $41\,309$ strictly positive integer witnesses, $23\,618$ equality residuals, $184\,016$ binary arithmetic gates, and exact total degree $12$. Every POWER and containment call is expanded into polynomial equations. Conditional on the explicitly inherited constructive Pell and matrix-interface theorems, its positive zeros are equivalent to membership of the direct-input target $T(x)$ in the associated directed semigroup generated by 195 fixed singular integer $7\times7$ matrices. An independent exact-source audit reconstructs every residual, macro argument, port, matrix map, and gate count; a separate interface audit reconstructs all 195 matrices and checks the proof boundaries.

The concrete contexts are \texttt{[110} and \texttt{A0]}. No arbitrary-program compiler is implemented, no universality of those particular contexts is inferred, and no improvement to the repository's prior 84-operation benchmark is claimed.

\medskip
\textbf{Reading guide.} Sections 1--3 state the exact language and dependencies. Sections 4--10 prove the positive-integer equivalence. Sections 11--14 give the literal ledger, independent evidence, reproducibility contract, and remaining questions. The appendix supplies notation and a complete outer-clause checklist.
\end{quote}

\wtagd\ \textbf{What is proved, and what is conditional.} Theorem~\ref{gts:cm:thm:main} has three layers, which the source keeps apart (Section~\ref{gts:cm:sec:scope}). The numerical theorem, that the literal polynomial recognizes the accepted inputs~$\mathcal A$ of the pinned countdown, is proved here relative to the two pinned mathlib theorems \texttt{Pell.matiyasevic} and \texttt{Pell.eq\_pow\_of\_pell}, which it specializes and does not re-prove. Its equivalence with membership of $T(x)$ in the 195-generator semigroup rests in addition on the research programme's faithful-group, lower-marker, context-transfer and synchronized-row theorems, which Section~\ref{gts:cm:sec:matrix} summarizes and does not re-prove. The program-family reading needs further the Neary--Woods simulation premise and an arbitrary-program compiler, which is not implemented. The polynomial is for one fixture, the contexts \texttt{[110} and \texttt{A0]}; it is not a universal polynomial.

\wtagd\ \textbf{Relation to Part~V.} The semigroup of this Part succeeds Part~V's. The research programme reduced Part~V's 229 generators to 193 (note at Part~V's question~4); this Part uses that 193-generator directed semigroup recoded faithfully in $\Gamma_1(5)$, with the two contexts absorbed into the matrices and a counted suffix that makes the input ordinary, which gives 195 singular integer $7\times7$ matrices (Section~\ref{gts:cm:sec:matrix}). Part~V certifies bounded lengths, one polynomial for each length; this Part gives one polynomial of fixed arity for histories of every length, and so answers Part~V's question~7 in part (note there). Section~\ref{gts:sec:relation} lists its other relations, and Table~\ref{gts:cm:tab:letters}, below, the letters that mean something else in Parts~I--V; it is printed here rather than in Section~\ref{gts:sec:notation} so that no earlier table number moves.

\section{The exact represented language}
\label{gts:cm:sec:language}
We write $\N=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$ and $\Pos=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}$. The supplied input is $x\in\Pos$. It is neither a shifted code $x-1$ nor a freely supplied Pell index. A countdown state has two signed row vectors and one signed counter,
\[
 (X,Y,n)\in\Z^2\times\Z^2\times\Z.
\]
Its fixed initial state is
\begin{equation}\label{gts:cm:eq:initial}
 X=(35\,426\,321,-19\,628\,667),\qquad Y=(1,0),\qquad n=x.
\end{equation}
The fixed matrix used by the loader is
\begin{equation}\label{gts:cm:eq:B}
 B=\begin{pmatrix}-52109&29036\\-94920&52891\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 B^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}52891&-29036\\94920&-52109\end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
There are exactly 97 branch choices:
\begin{align}
 \LOAD &: X'=X,\quad Y'=YB^{-1},\quad n'=n-1,\label{gts:cm:eq:load}\\
 \TILE_i &: X'=XK_i,\quad Y'=YG_i,\quad n=n'=0.
 \label{gts:cm:eq:tile}
\end{align}
The matrices $(K_i,G_i)$ are the actual 96 pairs in the pinned countdown receipt \cite{cm-countdown}. Row-vector multiplication is on the right. The accepting endpoint is
\begin{equation}\label{gts:cm:eq:endpoint}
 X=Y,\qquad n=0.
\end{equation}
No independent choice is permitted for the two row lanes. The common $i$ is essential to the matrix equivalence.

\begin{definition}
Let $\mathcal A\subseteq\Pos$ contain precisely those inputs for which the state \eqref{gts:cm:eq:initial} has a finite chronological path under \eqref{gts:cm:eq:load}--\eqref{gts:cm:eq:tile} reaching \eqref{gts:cm:eq:endpoint}. A path is a sequence of states with consecutive endpoints identified, not merely a multiset of locally valid edges.
\end{definition}

The retained original tile identifiers are
\[
 \{1,2,18,19\}\ \cup\ \{21,\ldots,107\}\ \cup\ \{109,110,111,112,114\}.
\]
The source numbers branch zero as $\LOAD$ and its other 96 branches according to the receipt's retained tile order. The complete numerical arrays, not small substitutes, are included in the release. They are data for a fixed polynomial; neither the input nor a witness selects a previously unpaid coefficient table.

\begin{theorem}[Literal fixed-arity certificate]\label{gts:cm:thm:main}
There is the explicitly supplied polynomial
\[
 \mathcal P(x,w_1,\ldots,w_{41309})\in\Z[x,w_1,\ldots,w_{41309}]
\]
with exact total degree $12$ such that, for every $x\in\Pos$,
\begin{equation}\label{gts:cm:eq:main}
 x\in\mathcal A\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists(w_1,\ldots,w_{41309})\in\Pos^{41309}\quad\mathcal P(x,w)=0.
\end{equation}
The declared literal implementation has $184\,016$ binary gates: $72\,093$ multiplications, $64\,111$ additions, and $47\,812$ subtractions. Fixed integer literals are free, while nontrivial fixed-coefficient multiplications occurring in the arithmetic are charged. The output is exactly the unweighted sum of the squares of $23\,618$ residuals.

The arithmetic semantics use the pinned constructive Pell theorems stated in Section~\ref{gts:cm:sec:power}. Under the inherited faithful-group, lower-marker, and synchronized-row interfaces in Section~\ref{gts:cm:sec:matrix}, \eqref{gts:cm:eq:main} is equivalent to membership of $T(x)$ in the associated fixed 195-generator singular $7\times7$ directed semigroup.
\end{theorem}

This is an implementation theorem. It asserts neither a shortest arithmetic circuit nor a bound on the bit size of witnesses. Its positive-integer domain is part of the theorem. In particular, degree twelve alone supplies no corresponding real-witness equivalence.

\section{What is fixed and what was previously unpaid}
\label{gts:cm:sec:scope}
The fixed contexts in this exact numerical instance are
\[
 U=\texttt{[110},\qquad V=\texttt{A0]}.
\]
They determine the context-transferred matrix array. The finite word that motivates the ordinary-input family is $U(W_0^2)^xV$, with $W_0=\texttt{01010111}$. This is an actual fixed-context family; it is not an implemented compiler from arbitrary programs.

The predecessor countdown source has a complete local transition polynomial of exact degree 194 and 2,065 gates, together with a seven-gate endpoint polynomial \cite{cm-countdown}. For fixed duration $h$, copying that local schedule gives a $2066h+7$ gate upper bound with $5h$ signed trajectory witnesses. These are valid length-dependent ledgers, but existentially naming $h$ does not turn $5h$ coordinates into a fixed list of variables.

Report55 supplies the missing arbitrary-duration packing. It does not insert the degree-194 polynomial as one free gate. Instead it certifies every selected affine branch through paid digit slices and four packed signed row updates. This different representation has a larger fixed circuit and a lower exact degree. The two resource comparisons must not be confused.
